> **Approach: rational resampling via interpolation on the upsampled grid.**
> 
> Simplest verifiable formulation:
> - Maintain a buffer of input samples (float64) plus a read position expressed in input samples.
> - Resample by: for each output sample index n (global), input position = n * down / up (in input samples, since src→dst means src_rate/dst_rate = down/up).
> - Use linear interpolation between floor and ceil input samples, on a signal that has been low-pass filtered to the target Nyquist to avoid aliasing.
> 
> The low-pass filtering: apply an FIR with cutoff at target Nyquist (0.5 * dst_rate/src_rate normalized) to the incoming stream (stateful), then linear-interpolate. For integer ratios, linear interpolation at exact sample positions = simple decimation. This is simple, correct-ish, and easy to verify.

          def _transcribe(self, frames: list[bytes]) -> str:
              min_frames = int((self.settings.min_speech_seconds * self.settings.sample_rate) / self.settings.block_size)
              if len(frames) < min_frames:
                  self._set_state(AssistantState.idle, last_transcript="")
                  return ""
      
              self._set_state(AssistantState.processing)
              recognizer = KaldiRecognizer(self._vosk_model, self.settings.sample_rate)
              for frame in frames:
                  recognizer.AcceptWaveform(frame)
      
              try:
                  final = json.loads(recognizer.FinalResult())
              except json.JSONDecodeError:
                  self._set_state(AssistantState.error, last_error="Invalid JSON from Vosk recognizer.")
                  return ""
      
              transcript = (final.get("text") or "").strip()
              self._set_state(AssistantState.processing, last_transcript=transcript)
              return transcript
      
          def _process_text(self, transcript: str) -> dict[str, Any]:
              self._set_state(AssistantState.processing, last_transcript=transcript)
      
              if not self.settings.groq_api_key:
                  error = "GROQ_API_KEY is missing."
                  self._set_state(AssistantState.error, last_error=error)
                  return {"error": error}
      
              llm = self._call_llm(transcript)
              spoken_reply = llm.get("spoken_reply", "").strip()
              action = llm.get("action", {"name": "none", "args": {}})
              action_result = self._execute_action(action)
              final_reply = spoken_reply
              if action_result:
                  final_reply = f"{spoken_reply} {action_result}".strip()
      
              self._set_state(AssistantState.speaking, last_reply=final_reply, last_action=action.get("name", "none"))
              self._speak(final_reply)
              self._set_state(AssistantState.idle)
      
              return {
                  "transcript": transcript,
                  "spoken_reply": spoken_reply,
                  "action": action,
                  "action_result": action_result,
                  "final_reply": final_reply,
              }
      
          def _call_llm(self, transcript: str) -> dict[str, Any]:
              endpoint = f"{self.settings.groq_base_url.rstrip('/')}/chat/completions"
              payload = {
                  "model": self.settings.groq_model,
                  "temperature": 0.3,
                  "response_format": {"type": "json_object"},
                  "messages": [
                      {"role": "system", "content": SYSTEM_PROMPT},
                      {"role": "user", "content": transcript},
                  ],
              }
              headers = {
                  "Authorization": f"Bearer {self.settings.groq_api_key}",
                  "Content-Type": "application/json",
              }
              response = self._client.post(endpoint, headers=headers, json=payload)
              response.raise_for_status()
              data = response.json()
      
              content = (
                  data.get("choices", [{}])[0]
                  .get("message", {})
                  .get("content", "")
              )
      
              parsed = self._extract_llm_json(content)
              if not isinstance(parsed, dict):
                  return {"spoken_reply": "No pude procesar la respuesta del modelo.", "action": {"name": "none", "args": {}}}
              if "spoken_reply" not in parsed:
                  parsed["spoken_reply"] = "No tengo respuesta ahora mismo."
              if "action" not in parsed or not isinstance(parsed["action"], dict):
                  parsed["action"] = {"name": "none", "args": {}}
              parsed["action"].setdefault("name", "none")
              parsed["action"].setdefault("args", {})
              return parsed
      
          @staticmethod
          def _extract_llm_json(content: str) -> dict[str, Any]:

                  return {}

      class VoiceAssistantRuntime:
          def __init__(self, settings: Settings):
              self.settings = settings
              self.state = RuntimeState()
              self._state_lock = threading.Lock()
              self._thread: threading.Thread | None = None
              self._stop_event = threading.Event()
              self._model = self._load_wakeword_model()
              self._vosk_model = self._load_vosk_model()
              self._client = httpx.Client(timeout=self.settings.groq_timeout_seconds)
              self._led = LedRingController(settings) if settings.led_enabled else None
      
          def _load_wakeword_model(self) -> OpenWakeWordModel:
              # Prefer ONNX to avoid hard dependency on tflite-runtime on some platforms.
              if self.settings.wakeword_model_path:
                  model_path = Path(self.settings.wakeword_model_path)
                  if not model_path.exists():
                      raise FileNotFoundError(f"Wake word model path not found: {model_path}")
                  self._ensure_openwakeword_resources()
                  return OpenWakeWordModel(
                      wakeword_models=[str(model_path)],
                      inference_framework="onnx" if str(model_path).endswith(".onnx") else "tflite",
                  )
      
              model_name = self.settings.wakeword_model_name or "hey_jarvis"
              try:
                  return OpenWakeWordModel(
                      wakeword_models=[model_name],
                      inference_framework="onnx",
                  )
              except Exception:
                  # Missing packaged resources are common in some installs; fetch only needed models.
                  openwakeword_download_models(model_names=[model_name])

> ```python
> class _Resampler:
>     """Band-limited converter from the microphone's native rate to 16 kHz.

>     Most USB microphones capture at 48 kHz (or 44.1 kHz) only, while openWakeWord

>     hear anything at all. This converts the captured blocks instead, with a
>     windowed-sinc (Hamming) polyphase bank: for an integer ratio it degenerates
>     to an exact anti-aliased decimation, which is the 48000 -> 16000 path this
>     Pi takes. State is kept between blocks, so the stream stays continuous and
>     no click appears at the block boundaries.

> _Reasoning_
> Two real failures found by the test — good that I tested:
> 
> 1. **44100 → 16000: 7 kHz is destroyed** (pico=57 instead of ~9000). Why? For 44100→16000, gcd=100 → up=160, down=441. The cutoff = 0.5 * min(1, 16000/44100)/up = 0.5*0.3628/160 = 0.001134. And `distance` = offsets - phase, where offsets are integers in *input* samples... 
> 
> Ah, I see the bug: the cutoff is expressed in units of the upsampled rate, but I'm evaluating `np.sinc(2*cutoff*distance)` where `distance` is in units of *input samples* (offsets are integer input-sample indices minus phase/up). In the polyphase formulation, the filter taps h[m] for output phase p should be `sinc(2*fc*(m*L - p))` where L = up. That is, the argument must be in units of the *upsampled* sample index: (m*up - p)/up... 
> 
> Let me redo the math. We want band-limited interpolation:
> y(t) = Σ_m x[m] · h(t - m) where h is a windowed sinc with cutoff fc (normalized to the *input* sample rate: fc = 0.5·min(1, dst/src) in cycles/input-sample).
> 
> So h(u) = 2·fc·sinc(2·fc·u), with u = t - m in input samples.
> 
> For an output at instant t = (cursor + k·down)/up input samples. Let me write t = s + f where s = floor(t) and f = fractional part = phase/up.
> 
> Then u = t - m. For m = s + j (j = offset from s), u = f - j.
> 
> So the tap for offset j is h(f - j) = 2·fc·sinc(2·fc·(f - j)), where fc = 0.5·min(1, dst/src).
> 
> My code computes `distance = offsets - phase` = j - f, and then `sinc(2*cutoff*distance)` with cutoff = 0.5·min(1,dst/src)/up. Two errors:
> - I divided the cutoff by `up` (wrong — no such division is needed under this formulation).
> - The sign: h(f-j) vs sinc(2·fc·(j-f)) — sinc is even, so sign doesn't matter.
> 
> So the fix: cutoff (in input-sample units) = 0.5·min(1, dst/src), and the argument = 2·cutoff·(distance), where distance = j - f = offsets - phase. Then scale by 2·cutoff... but the normalization by the sum handles gain.
> 
> Wait, but then for the 48000→16000 case (up=1, phase=0 always): distance = j (integers), cutoff = 0.5·(16000/48000) = 1/6. Argument = 2·(1/6)·j = j/3 → sinc(j/3) → zeros at j = ±3, ±6 ✓ → correct decimation kernel. And the test showed 48k works correctly (1k and 7k pass, 12k killed) — because for up=1 the division by up is a no-op! That's why only 44.1k failed.
> 
> So my formula was accidentally right for up=1 but wrong for up>1. The test caught it. 
> 
> Now the "exactitud" test also failed: error max 141% — that test compared y against a sine starting at t=0, but the resampler's first output corresponds to input instant 12 (half window), i.e. a time offset of 12/48000 = 0.25 ms → at 1 kHz that's a phase shift of 90°. So my reference alignment was wrong: I should compare against ref offset by the resampler's latency. The latency in *output* samples = half window (12 input samples = 4 output samples at 48k). Hmm, in output samples: the first output instant is at input time 12/48000 = 0.25ms; the k-th output at (12 + 3k)/48000 = 0.25ms + k/16000. So output[k] ≈ ref[k] shifted... i.e. output[k] corresponds to input time t_k = (12+3k)/48000. So compare y[k] with 12000·sin(2π·1000·t_k) where t_k = (12 + 3k)/48000 = (4 + k)/16000.
> 
> So ref[k] = 12000·sin(2π·1000·(k+4)/16000). My test used t=(k+1)/16000 — wrong by 3 output samples (≈68°). That explains the huge "error". So the accuracy test was misaligned, not necessarily the resampler.
> 
> Also should account for the FIR group delay: the polyphase design is centered (offsets symmetric, and for phase 0 the kernel is symmetric) → for up=1 the group delay is 0 relative to the phase reference... For the sinc interpolation formulation with a window centered at the output instant, there's no extra delay. Good.
> 
> So: fix the cutoff bug, then re-run the test with the correctly aligned reference and check the real numerical error.
> 
> Also, for 44.1k: after the fix, the cutoff = 0.5·(16000/44100) = 0.1814 → passband up to ~0.18·44100 = 8 kHz ✓, so 7 kHz passes.
> 
> Note: with cutoff in input-sample units, the filter length should scale with the decimation ratio for good quality: taps=25 at up=160 (44.1k) means 25 input samples ≈ 0.57 ms → a fairly wide transition band, and with phase offsets spaced 1/160 input samples the window length 25 input samples is fine.
> 
> Hmm, one concern: for the 44100 case, `np.where(|distance| <= half, hamming, 0)` — distance = j - phase ranges over [-half-1, half], |distance| <= half ok.
> 
> Let me now fix the code:
> 
> ```python
>         # Windowed-sinc interpolation kernel, cutoff at the lower of the two
>         # Nyquist limits (in units of the input sample rate) so nothing above
>         # the target band folds back when decimating.
>         cutoff = 0.5 * min(1.0, float(dst_rate) / float(src_rate))
>         phase = np.arange(self._up, dtype=np.float64) / self._up
>         distance = self._offsets.astype(np.float64)[None, :] - phase[:, None]
>         window = np.where(
>             np.abs(distance) <= self._half,
>             0.54 + 0.46 * np.cos(np.pi * distance / self._half),
>             0.0,
>         )
>         bank = np.sinc(2.0 * cutoff * distance) * window
>         bank *= 2.0 * cutoff
>         bank /= np.sum(bank, axis=1, keepdims=True)
> ```
> The `bank *= 2*cutoff` then renormalize by the sum → the sum normalization already ensures unity DC gain, so the 2*cutoff factor is redundant. Keep it simple: just normalize by the sum.
> 
> Hmm, careful with the Hamming window: `0.54 + 0.46*cos(pi*x/half)`. At x=0: 0.54+0.46 = 1.0 ✓. At |x|=half: 0.54-0.46 = 0.08 ✓ (Hamming at the edges = 0.08). Good.
> 
> Wait: standard Hamming is 0.54 + 0.46·cos(π·x/half) over x ∈ [-half, half] → yes, that's a raised cosine with value 0.08 at the ends. ✓
> 
> Also: with the corrected cutoff, the DC gain normalization per phase: sum of 2·fc·sinc(2·fc·(j-f)) over j ≈ 1 for all f (by construction, band-limited interpolation). Normalizing each phase by its own sum forces exact unity DC per phase — fine.
> 
> Let me also double check the integer-decimation case after the fix: cutoff = 1/6, distance = j (integers, up=1) → h[j] = sinc(j/3) ✓ zeros at ±3, ±6 → decimation by 3 has no aliasing from the decimation grid ✓.
> 
> Now let me also reconsider the numerical accuracy test alignment, and re-run.
> 
> Also should I bump `taps`? With taps=25 at up=1, the anti-alias filter is sinc(j/3)·hamming(25 taps) → it's a 25-tap filter with cutoff 1/6 of 48k = 8 kHz. Transition width is moderate; 12 kHz was attenuated to 28/12000 = -52 dB ✓. Good.
> 
> For 44.1k, taps=25 with a cutoff at 0.1814 (normalized to input rate) → 25 input samples ≈ 0.57 ms; the filter's transition band will be wide-ish. Test will tell whether 7000 passes with enough amplitude — the passband edge at ~0.18*44100... Let me just re-run and see the numbers.
>

If it's attenuated below ~7000, I'll increase taps based on the ratio: taps ∝ up (i.e., make the window span a fixed number of output samples). Standard practice: filter length ~ 8-16 output samples → taps = ~16*up. For up=160 that's 2560 taps (bank 160×2560 = 410k floats = 1.6 MB) — heavy but tolerable? The per-block cost becomes 1280 outputs × 2560 taps = 3.3 M MAC per 80 ms block = 41 M MAC/s — too much for a Pi 3.

But Hann has a wider main lobe... For interpolation quality, the standard choice is a windowed sinc; either is fine. Since the ends now go to zero, aliasing sidelobes are lower. > _Reasoning_
> Huge progress:
> - `health: {"ok":true,"runtime_running":true,"state":"idle"}` — the runtime is running (auto-started by the Java UI) ✅
> - state: wake_word_model "hey_jarvis_v0.1", wake_word_score small (3.7e-6, 6.4e-6) and **changing** → the mic stream is flowing ✅ (silence → below the RMS gate, so score 0... hmm, actually score is tiny non-zero; it's set when rms >= wake_min_rms)
> - last_error: "Audio overflow on input stream." — this is a concern: `stream.read()` returns overflowed=True → the code sets state to error on overflow. Note the state is still "idle" (the error is set via `_set_state(AssistantState.error, ...)` on overflow, then immediately after `_set_state(AssistantState.idle, ...)` in the same loop iteration → so state ends up idle but `last_error` stays recorded).
> 
> The overflow means the audio capture is dropping samples: the app isn't reading fast enough or the block size/buffering is insufficient. On a Pi 3 with a 3840-sample block at 48 kHz (80 ms), plus the resampler cost (2.66 ms per block = 3.3%), the loop must also run the wake-word ONNX inference. Hmm, but overflow also happened before my change? Before, the stream never opened at all. So we can't compare.
> 
> Is overflow harmful? Dropouts could clip speech, but with 80 ms blocks and PortAudio's default buffering, occasional overflow is tolerable-ish. However `last_error` showing an overflow is not clean. Let me check the frequency of overflows.
> 
> Actually, wait: the overflow is detected in `_run_loop` where after reading: `if overflowed: self._set_state(AssistantState.error, last_error="Audio overflow on input stream.")`. Then continues. So it's transient.
> 
> Why would it overflow? Because `_read_chunk` in the resampling path may need to read 2 blocks in one call at the start, and generally the loop does: read(3840) → resample (2.66ms) → wake word inference (ONNX, maybe 20-60 ms on Pi 3!) → next read. If the ONNX inference takes longer than the block duration (80 ms), the capture buffer overflows.
> 
> Wake-word inference on Pi 3 with onnxruntime: melspectrogram + embedding + the wake model — could easily be 50–150 ms. The energy gate (VOICE_WAKE_MIN_RMS=150) skips inference when quiet, which is the common case. But with a live mic in a room, RMS may exceed 150 often... Actually 150 RMS is quite low (near-silence is ~50-100). Room noise may exceed it frequently → inference runs → overflow.
>

>

With a PortAudio buffer of, say, 500 ms, overflow occurs every ~6 s... over 30 s, ~5 overflow events → 17 overflowed reads (each overflow flag may persist across several reads until the buffer drains).
>

> 
> How to fix properly?
> 
> Root cause: processing per block (2.7 ms resample + overhead) exceeds the *slack* available. Actually there IS no slack: PortAudio's read blocks until the block is ready; then processing happens while the device keeps filling its buffer. If processing time > 0, we're always slightly behind, and the deficit accumulates unless PortAudio's buffer absorbs it... 
> 
> Hmm wait, that's not right either. Let me think again very carefully.
> 
> PortAudio's input ring buffer size is finite (B ms). Each read(3840 frames) returns 3840 frames. Our loop's period = time between successive read completions. If our period > 80 ms, we consume slower than the device produces → the buffer drains? No — we produce data INTO the app; the device fills the buffer; if the app consumes slower than the device produces, the buffer OVERFLOWS (data lost). Yes: app consumption rate must be ≥ device production rate.

> Our period is ~88 ms to consume 80 ms of audio → consumption is slower → the buffer fills → overflow. Each overflow loses some audio. En Python 3.13 eso **falla en\nsilencio** (pip resuelve de forma atómica y `openwakeword` arrastra\n`tflite-runtime`, que no tiene ruedas). ## Qué quedó verificado

- **Migración**: jar ARM + `deploy/` + `voice-backend/` con modelos copiados, venv completo, ambos servicios `enabled`+`active`, autologin puesto, UI kiosco arrancando (`Alpha UI up in 23530 ms (kiosk=true)`) y **0 SEVERE** en el journal.
- **Fix del micrófono** (`voice-backend/main.py`): el USB PnP solo captura a 48 kHz; añadí `_Resampler` + fallback, medido en la propia Pi — **0,21 % de error** frente a la senoidal ideal, 1280 muestras/bloque estables, −52 dB / −75 dB sobre 12 kHz, 2,66 ms por bloque.
- **Gate ajustado** a la sala (`VOICE_WAKE_MIN_RMS=1000` en `~/.alpha/alpha.env`): 4,7 % de un núcleo en vez de saturar.
- **Un overflow ya no deja el orbe clavado en rojo.**

## Qué falló o no se pudo ejecutar

1. **5 % de audio perdido** (bucle a 88 ms por bloque de 80 ms): medido, no resuelto. La medición que lo aislaba (leer sin procesar) **falló** con `ALSA error -71` al pedir `blocksize=1280`, y no la repetí con 3840. Queda como el primer pendiente, con las dos hipótesis escritas en `MIGRACION-PI-ESTADO.md`.
2. **Reboot real**: no lo he hecho (dijiste que lo harías tú). Sí está verificado que todo está habilitado para arrancar solo.
3. **Voz de extremo a extremo**: nadie ha hablado al micro; los modelos, el stream y el estado están verificados, pero el ciclo *"hey Jarvis → respuesta"* no.
4. **Corrupción ext4 en la SD**: sólo evidencia y un parche puntual (`libportaudio.so.2.0.0` extraído del `.deb`). Sin reparar: necesita `fsck` offline o reflash.
5. **Docs sin sincronizar** (`PIAGENT.md`, `MIGRATIONTUTORIAL.md`, `push-to-pi.sh --setup`): siguen recomendando el `pip install -r requirements.txt` que falla en silencio en Python 3.13.
